========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, um, yeah, thank you for seeing me. I... well, it's work, honestly. I work at a meat processing plant, right? And it's a good job, I just got my bachelor's degree and I'm trying to get on my feet, but... I don't know, it's just getting really bad. What brings me in is that I keep starting things and just... not finishing them. Like, important things. At the plant, we have all these strict sanitation steps, which obviously you have to do, because it's meat, you know? You can't just skip cleaning the bandsaw or the grinders. And I know that! I know how important it is. But I'll start the sanitation process, I'll get all the chemicals out, I'll do the first few steps, and then somehow I just... leave out the last parts. Or the batch logs! We have to track every single batch of poultry that comes through my line, and I'll start writing it down, and then I just... don't finish the paperwork at the end of the line. It's like my brain just checks out before the very end, even though I know my job literally depends on finishing these things correctly. If the USDA inspector comes in and my logs are half-empty, or the equipment isn't fully sanitized, I could get fired, or the plant could get fined. It's so stressful, and I just don't understand why I keep doing it when I know how high the stakes are.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, it’s definitely not just a recent thing. It goes way, way back to when I was little. It’s actually kind of funny you ask about school, because my mom and I were just talking about this. She’s one of those people who keeps absolutely everything, you know? Like she has these giant plastic tote bins in her basement just full of my old school stuff, and she still has my elementary school report cards saved in there. We were looking at the ones from around fourth grade, and it was honestly a little hard to read, because the teacher wrote the exact same thing over and over. She kept saying that I was a really bright kid and that I had a lot of potential, but that I needed constant, and I mean *constant*, reminders to actually finish my classwork and turn in my homework. 

And looking back, I remember exactly how that felt, because it was so incredibly frustrating. I wasn't struggling to learn the material, you know? I vividly remember sitting at my desk and completely understanding the lessons, like the math concepts or whatever we were reading. I got it. But then the teacher would start giving out the instructions for what we were supposed to do next, and it was like my brain would just drop entire chunks of what she was saying. I would just miss huge pieces of the directions. The only way I ever really knew what was going on or managed to get through an assignment was if the teacher physically walked over and stayed close to my desk. If she was standing right there, keeping me on track, I could do it. But the second she walked away to help someone else, I’d just lose the thread and leave the worksheet half-finished, which is exactly what I'm doing now with the sanitation steps at the plant. So, yeah, it's been happening for a really, really long time, even though the stakes were obviously a lot lower back in fourth grade than they are now with the USDA.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, no, not really. Honestly, I've never really been the type to bounce off the walls or anything like that. Even when I was a kid, you know, when a lot of kids are just naturally hyper, I wasn't really like that. I remember my brother, actually, he was always getting in trouble for running around the house, knocking things over, just constantly needing to move. My mom would always say he had ants in his pants. But me? I was usually just sitting somewhere, maybe staring out the window or getting lost in my own head. I didn't really have that physical... energy, I guess you'd call it? 

And even now, at the plant, I don't really feel physically restless. I mean, the job itself is pretty physical—you're standing on the line, you're moving things around, so maybe that helps? But I don't feel like I *have* to move, or like I can't sit still if I need to. When I'm on my break, I can just sit in the breakroom and look at my phone or whatever without feeling like I need to get up and pace. 

As for the interrupting or blurting things out... no, I don't think so. I'm actually pretty quiet, especially at work. I tend to keep to myself a lot. Sometimes I feel like I'm a little too quiet, honestly, because I'll be in a meeting with the floor managers or something, and I'll have a thought, but I just won't say it. I definitely don't have that problem where I just blurt things out before I can stop myself. If anything, it's the opposite. I overthink what I'm going to say, or I just zone out and don't say anything at all. So, yeah, the physical restlessness and the impulsive talking... that's never really been a part of it for me. It's really just the... the mental drift, I guess, where I just lose track of what I'm doing and can't seem to finish the things I start, no matter how hard I try to force myself.

I didn't really have that physical... energy, I guess you'd call it? \n\nAnd even now, at the plant, I don't really feel physically restless. I mean, the job itself is pretty physical—you're standing on the line, you're moving things around, so maybe that helps? But I don't feel like I *have* to move, or like I can't sit still if I need to. When I'm on my break, I can just sit in the breakroom and look at my phone or whatever without feeling like I need to get up and pace. \n\nAs for the interrupting or blurting things out... no, I don't think so. I'm actually pretty quiet, especially at work. I tend to keep to myself a lot. Sometimes I feel like I'm a little too quiet, honestly, because I'll be in a meeting with the floor managers or something, and I'll have a thought, but I just won't say it. I definitely don't have that problem where I just blurt things out before I can stop myself. If anything, it's the opposite. I overthink what I'm going to say, or I just zone out and don't say anything at all. So, yeah, the physical restlessness and the impulsive talking... that's never really been a part of it for me. It's really just the... the mental drift, I guess, where I just lose track of what I'm doing and can't seem to finish the things I start, no matter how hard I try to force myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Oh my gosh, yes. Losing things is... it’s honestly the bane of my existence right now. It happens all the time, especially at the plant, which makes everything so much harder. Because, you know, working around heavy machinery and raw poultry, you have all this specific gear you absolutely *have* to have on you at all times. You can't just walk out there without it. But I am constantly—and I mean constantly—losing things I need on the floor.

I will literally take it off for two seconds to, I don't know, adjust my hairnet or try to fix a label, and then I’ll look down and it’s just... gone. Or my dry-erase marker for the whiteboards, or my employee badge, or my safety glasses! I will swear up and down that I *just* had them in my hand. Like, I can literally feel the phantom weight of the marker in my fingers, and I'll be thinking, "I just used this ten seconds ago," but I look down and my hand is empty. 

And then I have to stop whatever I’m doing, which is terrible because the line doesn't stop, you know? The meat just keeps coming. But I have to spend like ten minutes just walking in circles, retracing my steps. I'll be looking under the stainless steel prep tables, checking by the boot-washing stations, looking on top of the chemical drums, just completely panicked. 

Actually, just the other day—this was so ridiculous—I had to scan my badge to get into the main processing room. I scanned it, heard the beep, walked in, and by the time I got to my actual station on the line, the badge was missing. How do you lose a badge in a fifty-foot

What brings me in is that I keep starting things and just... not finishing them. Like, important things. At the plant, we have all these strict sanitation steps, which obviously you have to do, because it's meat, you know? You can't just skip cleaning the bandsaw or the grinders. And I know that! I know how important it is. But I'll start the sanitation process, I'll get all the chemicals out, I'll do the first few steps, and then somehow I just... leave out the last parts. Or the batch logs! We have to track every single batch of poultry that comes through my line, and I'll start writing it down, and then I just... don't finish the paperwork at the end of the line. It's like my brain just checks out before the very end, even though I know my job literally depends on finishing these things correctly. If the USDA inspector comes in and my logs are half-empty, or the equipment isn't fully sanitized, I could get fired, or the plant could get fined. It's so stressful, and I just don't understand why I keep doing it when I know how high the stakes are.\n\nDoctor: Did you notice this kind of trouble finishing tasks and staying focused on details back when you were a kid or in school, or is this something that's really only started recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it’s definitely not just a recent thing. It goes way, way back to when I was little. It’s actually kind of funny you ask about school, because my mom and I were just talking about this. She’s one of those people who keeps absolutely everything, you know? Like she has these giant plastic tote bins in her basement just full of my old school stuff, and she still has my elementary school report cards saved in there. We were looking at the ones from around fourth grade, and it was honestly a little hard to read, because the teacher wrote the exact same thing over and over. She kept saying that I was a really bright kid and that I had a lot of potential, but that I needed constant, and I mean *constant*, reminders to actually finish my classwork and turn in my homework. \n\nAnd looking back, I remember exactly how that felt, because it was so incredibly frustrating. I wasn't struggling to learn the material, you know? I vividly remember sitting at my desk and completely understanding the lessons, like the math concepts or whatever we were reading. I got it. But then the teacher would start giving out the instructions for what we were supposed to do next, and it was like my brain would just drop entire chunks of what she was saying. I would just miss huge pieces of the directions. The only way I ever really knew what was going on or managed to get through an assignment was if the teacher physically walked over and stayed close to my desk. If she was standing right there, keeping me on track, I could do it. But the second she walked away to help someone else, I’d just lose the thread and leave the worksheet half-finished, which is exactly what I'm doing now with the sanitation steps at the plant. So, yeah, it's been happening for a really, really long time, even though the stakes were obviously a lot lower back in fourth grade than they are now with the USDA.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble finishing tasks, do you notice things like feeling restless or fidgety, having trouble sitting still, or acting or speaking impulsively—interrupting others or blurting things out—either now or when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. Honestly, I've never really been the type to bounce off the walls or anything like that. Even when I was a kid, you know, when a lot of kids are just naturally hyper, I wasn't really like that. I remember my brother, actually, he was always getting in trouble for running around the house, knocking things over, just constantly needing to move. My mom would always say he had ants in his pants. But me? I was usually just sitting somewhere, maybe staring out the window or getting lost in my own head. I didn't really have that physical... energy, I guess you'd call it? \n\nAnd even now, at the plant, I don't really feel physically restless. I mean, the job itself is pretty physical—you're standing on the line, you're moving things around, so maybe that helps? But I don't feel like I *have* to move, or like I can't sit still if I need to. When I'm on my break, I can just sit in the breakroom and look at my phone or whatever without feeling like I need to get up and pace. \n\nAs for the interrupting or blurting things out... no, I don't think so. I'm actually pretty quiet, especially at work. I tend to keep to myself a lot. Sometimes I feel like I'm a little too quiet, honestly, because I'll be in a meeting with the floor managers or something, and I'll have a thought, but I just won't say it. I definitely don't have that problem where I just blurt things out before I can stop myself. If anything, it's the opposite. I overthink what I'm going to say, or I just zone out and don't say anything at all. So, yeah, the physical restlessness and the impulsive talking... that's never really been a part of it for me. It's really just the... the mental drift, I guess, where I just lose track of what I'm doing and can't seem to finish the things I start, no matter how hard I try to force myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing track of items you need, forgetting daily obligations, or getting easily distracted by things going on around you, both now and back when you were younger?\n\nPatient: Oh my gosh, yes. Losing things is... it’s honestly the bane of my existence right now. It happens all the time, especially at the plant, which makes everything so much harder. Because, you know, working around heavy machinery and raw poultry, you have all this specific gear you absolutely *have* to have on you at all times. You can't just walk out there without it. But I am constantly—and I mean constantly—losing things I need on the floor. \n\nLike my cut glove. You know, those heavy mesh ones we have to wear on our non-knife hand? I will literally take it off for two seconds to, I don't know, adjust my hairnet or try to fix a label, and then I’ll look down and it’s just... gone. Or my dry-erase marker for the whiteboards, or my employee badge, or my safety glasses! I will swear up and down that I *just* had them in my hand. Like, I can literally feel the phantom weight of the marker in my fingers, and I'll be thinking, \"I just used this ten seconds ago,\" but I look down and my hand is empty. \n\nAnd then I have to stop whatever I’m doing, which is terrible because the line doesn't stop, you know? The meat just keeps coming. But I have to spend like ten minutes just walking in circles, retracing my steps. I'll be looking under the stainless steel prep tables, checking by the boot-washing stations, looking on top of the chemical drums, just completely panicked. \n\nActually, just the other day—this was so ridiculous—I had to scan my badge to get into the main processing room. I scanned it, heard the beep, walked in, and by the time I got to my actual station on the line, the badge was missing. How do you lose a badge in a fifty-foot\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

When I'm on my break, I can just sit in the breakroom and look at my phone or whatever without feeling like I need to get up and pace. \n\nAs for the interrupting or blurting things out... no, I don't think so. I'm actually pretty quiet, especially at work. I tend to keep to myself a lot. Sometimes I feel like I'm a little too quiet, honestly, because I'll be in a meeting with the floor managers or something, and I'll have a thought, but I just won't say it. I definitely don't have that problem where I just blurt things out before I can stop myself. If anything, it's the opposite. I overthink what I'm going to say, or I just zone out and don't say anything at all. So, yeah, the physical restlessness and the impulsive talking... that's never really been a part of it for me. It's really just the... the mental drift, I guess, where I just lose track of what I'm doing and can't seem to finish the things I start, no matter how hard I try to force myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing track of items you need, forgetting daily obligations, or getting easily distracted by things going on around you, both now and back when you were younger?\n\nPatient: Oh my gosh, yes. Losing things is... it’s honestly the bane of my existence right now. It happens all the time, especially at the plant, which makes everything so much harder. Because, you know, working around heavy machinery and raw poultry, you have all this specific gear you absolutely *have* to have on you at all times. You can't just walk out there without it. But I am constantly—and I mean constantly—losing things I need on the floor. \n\nLike my cut glove. You know, those heavy mesh ones we have to wear on our non-knife hand? I will literally take it off for two seconds to, I don't know, adjust my hairnet or try to fix a label, and then I’ll look down and it’s just... gone. Or my dry-erase marker for the whiteboards, or my employee badge, or my safety glasses! I will swear up and down that I *just* had them in my hand. Like, I can literally feel the phantom weight of the marker in my fingers, and I'll be thinking, \"I just used this ten seconds ago,\" but I look down and my hand is empty. \n\nAnd then I have to stop whatever I’m doing, which is terrible because the line doesn't stop, you know? The meat just keeps coming. But I have to spend like ten minutes just walking in circles, retracing my steps. I'll be looking under the stainless steel prep tables, checking by the boot-washing stations, looking on top of the chemical drums, just completely panicked. \n\nActually, just the other day—this was so ridiculous—I had to scan my badge to get into the main processing room. I scanned it, heard the beep, walked in, and by the time I got to my actual station on the line, the badge was missing. How do you lose a badge in a fifty-foot\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling persistently sad, anxious, or worried lately, or noticed any changes in your sleep, appetite, or energy that might be affecting your concentration?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
...walk? I still have absolutely no idea where it went. They had to issue me a temporary one for the day. But, um, anyway, I'm sorry, to answer your question about feeling sad or anxious... no, not really. I mean, obviously I get incredibly frustrated with myself, like I was just saying with the badge thing, or when I leave a task half-finished on the floor, but it's not a deep sadness or depression or anything like that. I'm actually a fairly upbeat person most of the time! And I don't really sit around worrying about things, either. Well, I mean, I worry a little bit about my floor manager getting mad at me, but that feels like a pretty normal thing to worry about when you keep losing company property, you know? It's not like this heavy, constant anxiety that just hangs over me for no reason or keeps me up at night. 

And as for my sleep and energy, those are completely fine. Honestly, my sleep is probably the most normal thing about me right now. Because working at the plant is so physically demanding—you're standing on hard concrete for eight hours in these massive, heavy rubber boots with steel toes that just weigh your legs down—by the time I get home, I am just wiped out in a good way. I usually have to take a really long, hot shower just to get the smell of the industrial bleach and the raw poultry off my skin, because that smell just clings to absolutely everything, and then I just crash. I sleep like a rock, all the way through the night. So when my alarm goes off, I have plenty of energy. I'm not dragging myself out of bed or feeling exhausted during the day. 

And my appetite is definitely fine, too. If anything, working in that environment makes you hungrier, because they have to keep the main processing room at around 40 degrees for USDA food safety regulations, so your body is just constantly burning extra calories trying to stay warm under your smock. I always pack a really big lunch, usually a massive turkey sandwich and some pretzels, maybe a couple of those little mandarin oranges, and I eat absolutely all of it during my thirty-minute break. I never skip meals. So, yeah, I'm eating normally, sleeping normally, and I don't feel down. It really is just... just my brain completely refusing to hold onto a single thought or finish a task, no matter how much energy I have or how good my mood is.

through_Difficulty\n  description: Often does not follow through on instructions and fails to finish schoolwork, chores, or duties in the workplace.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that I keep starting the sanitation steps, batch logs, or end-of-line paperwork and somehow leave out the last parts, even when I know my job depends on finishing them correctly.\n- key: S006\n  name: Sustained_Mental_Effort_Avoidance\n  description: Often avoids, dislikes, or is reluctant to engage in tasks that require sustained mental effort.\n  manifestation: I put off the certification modules and any long procedure reading until the last possible minute because sitting there trying to absorb all those rules feels almost physically exhausting.\n- key: S007\n  name: Losing_Items\n  description: Often loses things necessary for tasks or activities.\n  manifestation: I’m constantly losing things I need on the floor, like my cut glove, marker, badge, or safety glasses, and I’ll swear I just had them in my hand before I spend ten minutes retracing my steps.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: My mom still has elementary school report cards from around fourth grade saying I was bright but needed constant reminders to finish classwork and turn in homework. I remember understanding the lessons but missing chunks of instructions unless the teacher stayed close to me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work this has led to formal retraining meetings, coworkers covering parts of my duties, and real fear that I’ll be moved off the line or fired. I feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of the reliable worker I want to be.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been a serious problem since I switched into full-time meat processing about two years and four months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S004\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble finishing tasks, do you notice things like feeling restless or fidgety, having trouble sitting still, or acting or speaking impulsively—interrupting others or blurting things out—either now or when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. Honestly, I've never really been the type to bounce off the walls or anything like that. Even when I was a kid, you know, when a lot of kids are just naturally hyper, I wasn't really like that. I remember my brother, actually, he was always getting in trouble for running around the house, knocking things over, just constantly needing to move. My mom would always say he had ants in his pants. But me? I was usually just sitting somewhere, maybe staring out the window or getting lost in my own head. I didn't really have that physical... energy, I guess you'd call it? \n\nAnd even now, at the plant, I don't really feel physically restless. I mean, the job itself is pretty physical—you're standing on the line, you're moving things around, so maybe that helps? But I don't feel like I *have* to move, or like I can't sit still if I need to. When I'm on my break, I can just sit in the breakroom and look at my phone or whatever without feeling like I need to get up and pace. \n\nAs for the interrupting or blurting things out... no, I don't think so. I'm actually pretty quiet, especially at work. I tend to keep to myself a lot. Sometimes I feel like I'm a little too quiet, honestly, because I'll be in a meeting with the floor managers or something, and I'll have a thought, but I just won't say it. I definitely don't have that problem where I just blurt things out before I can stop myself. If anything, it's the opposite. I overthink what I'm going to say, or I just zone out and don't say anything at all. So, yeah, the physical restlessness and the impulsive talking... that's never really been a part of it for me. It's really just the... the mental drift, I guess, where I just lose track of what I'm doing and can't seem to finish the things I start, no matter how hard I try to force myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing track of items you need, forgetting daily obligations, or getting easily distracted by things going on around you, both now and back when you were younger?\n\nPatient: Oh my gosh, yes. Losing things is... it’s honestly the bane of my existence right now. It happens all the time, especially at the plant, which makes everything so much harder. Because, you know, working around heavy machinery and raw poultry, you have all this specific gear you absolutely *have* to have on you at all times. You can't just walk out there without it. But I am constantly—and I mean constantly—losing things I need on the floor. \n\nLike my cut glove. You know, those heavy mesh ones we have to wear on our non-knife hand? I will literally take it off for two seconds to, I don't know, adjust my hairnet or try to fix a label, and then I’ll look down and it’s just... gone. Or my dry-erase marker for the whiteboards, or my employee badge, or my safety glasses! I will swear up and down that I *just* had them in my hand. Like, I can literally feel the phantom weight of the marker in my fingers, and I'll be thinking, \"I just used this ten seconds ago,\" but I look down and my hand is empty. \n\nAnd then I have to stop whatever I’m doing, which is terrible because the line doesn't stop, you know? The meat just keeps coming. But I have to spend like ten minutes just walking in circles, retracing my steps. I'll be looking under the stainless steel prep tables, checking by the boot-washing stations, looking on top of the chemical drums, just completely panicked. \n\nActually, just the other day—this was so ridiculous—I had to scan my badge to get into the main processing room. I scanned it, heard the beep, walked in, and by the time I got to my actual station on the line, the badge was missing. How do you lose a badge in a fifty-foot\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling persistently sad, anxious, or worried lately, or noticed any changes in your sleep, appetite, or energy that might be affecting your concentration?\n\nPatient: ...walk? I still have absolutely no idea where it went. They had to issue me a temporary one for the day. But, um, anyway, I'm sorry, to answer your question about feeling sad or anxious... no, not really. I mean, obviously I get incredibly frustrated with myself, like I was just saying with the badge thing, or when I leave a task half-finished on the floor, but it's not a deep sadness or depression or anything like that. I'm actually a fairly upbeat person most of the time! And I don't really sit around worrying about things, either. Well, I mean, I worry a little bit about my floor manager getting mad at me, but that feels like a pretty normal thing to worry about when you keep losing company property, you know? It's not like this heavy, constant anxiety that just hangs over me for no reason or keeps me up at night. \n\nAnd as for my sleep and energy, those are completely fine. Honestly, my sleep is probably the most normal thing about me right now. Because working at the plant is so physically demanding—you're standing on hard concrete for eight hours in these massive, heavy rubber boots with steel toes that just weigh your legs down—by the time I get home, I am just wiped out in a good way. I usually have to take a really long, hot shower just to get the smell of the industrial bleach and the raw poultry off my skin, because that smell just clings to absolutely everything, and then I just crash. I sleep like a rock, all the way through the night. So when my alarm goes off, I have plenty of energy. I'm not dragging myself out of bed or feeling exhausted during the day. \n\nAnd my appetite is definitely fine, too. If anything, working in that environment makes you hungrier, because they have to keep the main processing room at around 40 degrees for USDA food safety regulations, so your body is just constantly burning extra calories trying to stay warm under your smock. I always pack a really big lunch, usually a massive turkey sandwich and some pretzels, maybe a couple of those little mandarin oranges, and I eat absolutely all of it during my thirty-minute break. I never skip meals. So, yeah, I'm eating normally, sleeping normally, and I don't feel down. It really is just... just my brain completely refusing to hold onto a single thought or finish a task, no matter how much energy I have or how good my mood is.\n\n[Last Question]\nOutside of work, do you notice this same pattern—like losing things, forgetting responsibilities, or not finishing tasks—affecting things at home, such as paying bills, keeping appointments, or managing chores?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, I mean... I guess I don't really have a lot of personal errands or appointments to keep track of right now? Like, I just graduated, so I don't have classes or study groups or anything like that anymore. And since I started at the plant, my schedule is just so completely consumed by work and then recovering from work. 

I don't really go to the doctor much, except for, well, this. And as for plans with friends... my roommates are my main friends, and we're just kind of always around each other at the apartment anyway. We don't really make "plans," you know? We just sort of exist in the same space. Like, if we're going to watch a movie, it's just because we both happen to be sitting on the couch at the same time and someone turns the TV on. 

And my other friends from college, the ones who didn't move in with me, they all kind of scattered after graduation. Some of them moved back home, some of them got jobs in other cities, so we just text or whatever. I don't really have to remember to meet them somewhere at a specific time. 

I guess... I don't know, I just haven't really noticed it being a problem outside of work? At work, it's just so obvious because everything is so structured and fast-paced and there are all these rules and procedures, so when I mess up or forget something, it's immediately a huge deal. But in my personal life, things are just so much more loose and unstructured right now. If I forget to do something, or if I start something and don't finish it, there's no floor manager there to yell at me about it, you know? It just kind of... doesn't matter as much. So, yeah, I can't really think of any specific examples of it happening with errands or appointments or anything like that.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—thanks for coming today. Can you tell me what's been bringing you in to see me?\n\nPatient: Hi, um, yeah, thank you for seeing me. I... well, it's work, honestly. I work at a meat processing plant, right? And it's a good job, I just got my bachelor's degree and I'm trying to get on my feet, but... I don't know, it's just getting really bad. What brings me in is that I keep starting things and just... not finishing them. Like, important things. At the plant, we have all these strict sanitation steps, which obviously you have to do, because it's meat, you know? You can't just skip cleaning the bandsaw or the grinders. And I know that! I know how important it is. But I'll start the sanitation process, I'll get all the chemicals out, I'll do the first few steps, and then somehow I just... leave out the last parts. Or the batch logs! We have to track every single batch of poultry that comes through my line, and I'll start writing it down, and then I just... don't finish the paperwork at the end of the line. It's like my brain just checks out before the very end, even though I know my job literally depends on finishing these things correctly. If the USDA inspector comes in and my logs are half-empty, or the equipment isn't fully sanitized, I could get fired, or the plant could get fined. It's so stressful, and I just don't understand why I keep doing it when I know how high the stakes are.\n\nDoctor: Did you notice this kind of trouble finishing tasks and staying focused on details back when you were a kid or in school, or is this something that's really only started recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it’s definitely not just a recent thing. It goes way, way back to when I was little. It’s actually kind of funny you ask about school, because my mom and I were just talking about this. She’s one of those people who keeps absolutely everything, you know? Like she has these giant plastic tote bins in her basement just full of my old school stuff, and she still has my elementary school report cards saved in there. We were looking at the ones from around fourth grade, and it was honestly a little hard to read, because the teacher wrote the exact same thing over and over. She kept saying that I was a really bright kid and that I had a lot of potential, but that I needed constant, and I mean *constant*, reminders to actually finish my classwork and turn in my homework. \n\nAnd looking back, I remember exactly how that felt, because it was so incredibly frustrating. I wasn't struggling to learn the material, you know? I vividly remember sitting at my desk and completely understanding the lessons, like the math concepts or whatever we were reading. I got it. But then the teacher would start giving out the instructions for what we were supposed to do next, and it was like my brain would just drop entire chunks of what she was saying. I would just miss huge pieces of the directions. The only way I ever really knew what was going on or managed to get through an assignment was if the teacher physically walked over and stayed close to my desk. If she was standing right there, keeping me on track, I could do it. But the second she walked away to help someone else, I’d just lose the thread and leave the worksheet half-finished, which is exactly what I'm doing now with the sanitation steps at the plant. So, yeah, it's been happening for a really, really long time, even though the stakes were obviously a lot lower back in fourth grade than they are now with the USDA.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the trouble finishing tasks, do you notice things like feeling restless or fidgety, having trouble sitting still, or acting or speaking impulsively—interrupting others or blurting things out—either now or when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. Honestly, I've never really been the type to bounce off the walls or anything like that. Even when I was a kid, you know, when a lot of kids are just naturally hyper, I wasn't really like that. I remember my brother, actually, he was always getting in trouble for running around the house, knocking things over, just constantly needing to move. My mom would always say he had ants in his pants. But me? I was usually just sitting somewhere, maybe staring out the window or getting lost in my own head. I didn't really have that physical... energy, I guess you'd call it? \n\nAnd even now, at the plant, I don't really feel physically restless. I mean, the job itself is pretty physical—you're standing on the line, you're moving things around, so maybe that helps? But I don't feel like I *have* to move, or like I can't sit still if I need to. When I'm on my break, I can just sit in the breakroom and look at my phone or whatever without feeling like I need to get up and pace. \n\nAs for the interrupting or blurting things out... no, I don't think so. I'm actually pretty quiet, especially at work. I tend to keep to myself a lot. Sometimes I feel like I'm a little too quiet, honestly, because I'll be in a meeting with the floor managers or something, and I'll have a thought, but I just won't say it. I definitely don't have that problem where I just blurt things out before I can stop myself. If anything, it's the opposite. I overthink what I'm going to say, or I just zone out and don't say anything at all. So, yeah, the physical restlessness and the impulsive talking... that's never really been a part of it for me. It's really just the... the mental drift, I guess, where I just lose track of what I'm doing and can't seem to finish the things I start, no matter how hard I try to force myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing track of items you need, forgetting daily obligations, or getting easily distracted by things going on around you, both now and back when you were younger?\n\nPatient: Oh my gosh, yes. Losing things is... it’s honestly the bane of my existence right now. It happens all the time, especially at the plant, which makes everything so much harder. Because, you know, working around heavy machinery and raw poultry, you have all this specific gear you absolutely *have* to have on you at all times. You can't just walk out there without it. But I am constantly—and I mean constantly—losing things I need on the floor. \n\nLike my cut glove. You know, those heavy mesh ones we have to wear on our non-knife hand? I will literally take it off for two seconds to, I don't know, adjust my hairnet or try to fix a label, and then I’ll look down and it’s just... gone. Or my dry-erase marker for the whiteboards, or my employee badge, or my safety glasses! I will swear up and down that I *just* had them in my hand. Like, I can literally feel the phantom weight of the marker in my fingers, and I'll be thinking, \"I just used this ten seconds ago,\" but I look down and my hand is empty. \n\nAnd then I have to stop whatever I’m doing, which is terrible because the line doesn't stop, you know? The meat just keeps coming. But I have to spend like ten minutes just walking in circles, retracing my steps. I'll be looking under the stainless steel prep tables, checking by the boot-washing stations, looking on top of the chemical drums, just completely panicked. \n\nActually, just the other day—this was so ridiculous—I had to scan my badge to get into the main processing room. I scanned it, heard the beep, walked in, and by the time I got to my actual station on the line, the badge was missing. How do you lose a badge in a fifty-foot\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling persistently sad, anxious, or worried lately, or noticed any changes in your sleep, appetite, or energy that might be affecting your concentration?\n\nPatient: ...walk? I still have absolutely no idea where it went. They had to issue me a temporary one for the day. But, um, anyway, I'm sorry, to answer your question about feeling sad or anxious... no, not really. I mean, obviously I get incredibly frustrated with myself, like I was just saying with the badge thing, or when I leave a task half-finished on the floor, but it's not a deep sadness or depression or anything like that. I'm actually a fairly upbeat person most of the time! And I don't really sit around worrying about things, either. Well, I mean, I worry a little bit about my floor manager getting mad at me, but that feels like a pretty normal thing to worry about when you keep losing company property, you know? It's not like this heavy, constant anxiety that just hangs over me for no reason or keeps me up at night. \n\nAnd as for my sleep and energy, those are completely fine. Honestly, my sleep is probably the most normal thing about me right now. Because working at the plant is so physically demanding—you're standing on hard concrete for eight hours in these massive, heavy rubber boots with steel toes that just weigh your legs down—by the time I get home, I am just wiped out in a good way. I usually have to take a really long, hot shower just to get the smell of the industrial bleach and the raw poultry off my skin, because that smell just clings to absolutely everything, and then I just crash. I sleep like a rock, all the way through the night. So when my alarm goes off, I have plenty of energy. I'm not dragging myself out of bed or feeling exhausted during the day. \n\nAnd my appetite is definitely fine, too. If anything, working in that environment makes you hungrier, because they have to keep the main processing room at around 40 degrees for USDA food safety regulations, so your body is just constantly burning extra calories trying to stay warm under your smock. I always pack a really big lunch, usually a massive turkey sandwich and some pretzels, maybe a couple of those little mandarin oranges, and I eat absolutely all of it during my thirty-minute break. I never skip meals. So, yeah, I'm eating normally, sleeping normally, and I don't feel down. It really is just... just my brain completely refusing to hold onto a single thought or finish a task, no matter how much energy I have or how good my mood is.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you notice this same pattern—like losing things, forgetting responsibilities, or not finishing tasks—affecting things at home, such as paying bills, keeping appointments, or managing chores?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly... I'm not really sure? I haven't really noticed it being an issue at home, to be completely honest. I mean, my living situation is kind of set up so I don't really have to worry about a lot of that stuff? I guess I don't really have a lot of personal errands or appointments to keep track of right now? Like, I just graduated, so I don't have classes or study groups or anything like that anymore. And since I started at the plant, my schedule is just so completely consumed by work and then recovering from work. \n\nI don't really go to the doctor much, except for, well, this. And as for plans with friends... my roommates are my main friends, and we're just kind of always around each other at the apartment anyway. We don't really make \"plans,\" you know? We just sort of exist in the same space. Like, if we're going to watch a movie, it's just because we both happen to be sitting on the couch at the same time and someone turns the TV on. \n\nAnd my other friends from college, the ones who didn't move in with me, they all kind of scattered after graduation. Some of them moved back home, some of them got jobs in other cities, so we just text or whatever. I don't really have to remember to meet them somewhere at a specific time. \n\nI guess... I don't know, I just haven't really noticed it being a problem outside of work? At work, it's just so obvious because everything is so structured and fast-paced and there are all these rules and procedures, so when I mess up or forget something, it's immediately a huge deal. But in my personal life, things are just so much more loose and unstructured right now. If I forget to do something, or if I start something and don't finish it, there's no floor manager there to yell at me about it, you know? It just kind of... doesn't matter as much. So, yeah, I can't really think of any specific examples of it happening with errands or appointments or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Because working at the plant is so physically demanding—you're standing on hard concrete for eight hours in these massive, heavy rubber boots with steel toes that just weigh your legs down—by the time I get home, I am just wiped out in a good way. I usually have to take a really long, hot shower just to get the smell of the industrial bleach and the raw poultry off my skin, because that smell just clings to absolutely everything, and then I just crash. I sleep like a rock, all the way through the night. So when my alarm goes off, I have plenty of energy. I'm not dragging myself out of bed or feeling exhausted during the day. \n\nAnd my appetite is definitely fine, too. If anything, working in that environment makes you hungrier, because they have to keep the main processing room at around 40 degrees for USDA food safety regulations, so your body is just constantly burning extra calories trying to stay warm under your smock. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, hobbies... well, that's the thing, right? I don't really have a lot of energy for hobbies right now, like I said, because the plant just takes it all out of me. But, um, even if I did... I guess what you're asking makes me think about what actually happens at work, because it's not just about losing my badge or walking away from a pallet. It's... it's the actual work itself, the actual tasks I have to do. 

Like, okay, so a big part of my job on the line is this sequence of things—cutting, weighing, and then checking the labels. And it's repetitive, obviously, because it's a processing plant, but you have to be really precise because of quality control and the USDA standards. And I will go into a shift, especially after I've had a talking-to from the floor manager, and I will tell myself, "Okay, today you are going to stay completely locked in. You are going to focus on every single cut, every single weight." And I try. I really, really try. 

But then... it's like my brain just can't hold onto it. After a while, all the motions—the cutting, the weighing, the checking the little printed labels—they just start to blur together. It's like the edges of the tasks just get fuzzy. And I'll suddenly snap out of it and realize I've just been standing there, staring at the same tray of chicken breasts or looking at the same log sheet on my clipboard for I don't even know how long, completely blanked out. Like, I'm looking at it, but I'm not taking in any of the information, and I have absolutely no idea what I'm supposed to be doing next or where I was in the process. 

It's so frustrating because I *want* to do a good job, and I start out with all this intention to just power through and pay attention, but the focus just... evaporates. It just slips right through my fingers, no matter how hard I try to grip it. So if I can't even maintain that kind of focus when my job and my paycheck are on the line, and when I'm actively trying to force myself to do it, I can't imagine I'd be any better at sticking with a book or a personal project where there are no stakes at all, you know? It's just... the staying with it, the keeping my brain engaged on one thing for an extended period of time... it just feels impossible right now.

Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nUm, no, not really. Honestly, I've never really been the type to bounce off the walls or anything like that. I remember my brother, actually, he was always getting in trouble for running around the house, knocking things over, just constantly needing to move. My mom would always say he had ants in his pants. But me? I was usually just sitting somewhere, maybe staring out the window or getting lost in my own head. I didn't really have that physical... energy, I guess you'd call it? \n\nAnd even now, at the plant, I don't really feel physically restless. I mean, the job itself is pretty physical—you're standing on the line, you're moving things around, so maybe that helps? But I don't feel like I *have* to move, or like I can't sit still if I need to. When I'm on my break, I can just sit in the breakroom and look at my phone or whatever without feeling like I need to get up and pace. \n\nAs for the interrupting or blurting things out... no, I don't think so. I'm actually pretty quiet, especially at work. I tend to keep to myself a lot. Sometimes I feel like I'm a little too quiet, honestly, because I'll be in a meeting with the floor managers or something, and I'll have a thought, but I just won't say it. I definitely don't have that problem where I just blurt things out before I can stop myself. If anything, it's the opposite. I overthink what I'm going to say, or I just zone out and don't say anything at all. So, yeah, the physical restlessness and the impulsive talking... that's never really been a part of it for me. It's really just the... the mental drift, I guess, where I just lose track of what I'm doing and can't seem to finish the things I start, no matter how hard I try to force myself.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n...walk? I still have absolutely no idea where it went. They had to issue me a temporary one for the day. But, um, anyway, I'm sorry, to answer your question about feeling sad or anxious... no, not really. I mean, obviously I get incredibly frustrated with myself, like I was just saying with the badge thing, or when I leave a task half-finished on the floor, but it's not a deep sadness or depression or anything like that. I'm actually a fairly upbeat person most of the time! And I don't really sit around worrying about things, either. Well, I mean, I worry a little bit about my floor manager getting mad at me, but that feels like a pretty normal thing to worry about when you keep losing company property, you know? It's not like this heavy, constant anxiety that just hangs over me for no reason or keeps me up at night. \n\nAnd as for my sleep and energy, those are completely fine. Honestly, my sleep is probably the most normal thing about me right now. Because working at the plant is so physically demanding—you're standing on hard concrete for eight hours in these massive, heavy rubber boots with steel toes that just weigh your legs down—by the time I get home, I am just wiped out in a good way. I usually have to take a really long, hot shower just to get the smell of the industrial bleach and the raw poultry off my skin, because that smell just clings to absolutely everything, and then I just crash. I sleep like a rock, all the way through the night. So when my alarm goes off, I have plenty of energy. I'm not dragging myself out of bed or feeling exhausted during the day. \n\nAnd my appetite is definitely fine, too. If anything, working in that environment makes you hungrier, because they have to keep the main processing room at around 40 degrees for USDA food safety regulations, so your body is just constantly burning extra calories trying to stay warm under your smock. I always pack a really big lunch, usually a massive turkey sandwich and some pretzels, maybe a couple of those little mandarin oranges, and I eat absolutely all of it during my thirty-minute break. I never skip meals. So, yeah, I'm eating normally, sleeping normally, and I don't feel down. It really is just... just my brain completely refusing to hold onto a single thought or finish a task, no matter how much energy I have or how good my mood is.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.
